```text
You’re right. The workflow now has to **listen/transcribe on our side**, not ask you to download anything.

I implemented that.

What now happens for audio-only finalist episodes:

1. **Download episode audio server-side**
   - From the RSS `audio_url`
   - Stored under `/opt/data/podcast_digest/transcripts/…`

2. **Transcribe locally**
   - STT engine: **faster-whisper**
   - Model default: **Whisper `base`**
   - Runtime: local CPU via `ctranslate2`
   - Venv: `/opt/data/venvs/podcast-stt`

3. **Chunk the transcript**
   - Transcript is split into ~12k-character chunks.

4. **Feed chunks into the digest model**
   - Chunk evidence extraction model: **OpenRouter / Qwen**, currently `qwen/qwen3-235b-a22b`
   - It extracts claims, arguments, evidence, counterpoints, and DJ-relevant takeaways.

5. **Write the final podcast digest**
   - Orchestration/editorial synthesis: me, currently **Codex 5.5**
   - The script now receives:
     - page transcript if available,
     - otherwise local audio transcript notes/excerpts,
     - otherwise only metadata as a last resort.

6. **Render audio**
   - TTS: local **Piper**
   - Not an intelligence model — just voice synthesis.

I verified it two ways:

- **Smoke test:** 30-second clipped transcription of a finalist audio-only podcast worked.
- **Full test:** full 941-second podcast audio transcribed successfully:

  - Duration: ~15.7 minutes

  - Transcript path: `/opt/data/podcast_digest/transcripts/96f8c9fe4a1db62c/transcript.txt`

Important detail: production default is now **full transcription**:

``​`text
PODCAST_WEEKLY_STT_MAX_SECONDS=0
``​`

So clips are only for smoke tests. The weekly production run should transcribe the full audio for finalists without transcripts.

{"content": "     1|MAYA: This week, we'll hear from Jensen Huang on NVIDIA's AI factories as the new dynamos of the intelligence age; from Dan Dreyfus on America's critical minerals crisis and its strategic implications; and from Rebecca Hinds on the hidden labor costs of enterprise AI adoption.\n     2|\n     3|SAM: Let's start with the first episode.\n     4|\n     5|### Episode 1: **Training Data** – *LIVE: Jensen Huang on Building the Dynamo of the Intelligence Age*\n     6|\n     7|MAYA: Jensen Huang, founder of NVIDIA, describes a fundamental shift in computing—from retrieval to generation—and positions NVIDIA’s AI factories as the new dynamos powering the next economic era. He outlines five layers of AI investment and argues that AI won’t destroy jobs but instead create new industries.\n     8|\n     9|SAM: The strongest argument he makes is that AI is the third cocooning force in history—like electricity and the internet—reshaping everything from energy to applications. He debunks fears of AI-driven job loss by pointing to historical parallels where new technologies created more demand than they displaced.\n    10|\n    11|MAYA: A counterpoint or open question: Will AI truly generate new categories of work at the same scale it automates existing ones, especially in the short term? There’s optimism, but also a risk of miscalculating labor transitions.\n    12|\n    13|SAM: Why should DJ care? Because this is a strategic narrative on how AI will power the next economic era. Huang’s framing of AI as infrastructure—not just a tool—has implications for how we think about capital allocation, platform strategy, and long-term industry shifts.\n    14|\n    15|---\n    16|\n    17|### Episode 2: **All-In Podcast** – *Dan Dreyfus: America's Critical Minerals Crisis is Here*\n    18|\n    19|MAYA: Dan Dreyfus outlines the looming crisis in critical minerals supply, driven by China’s dominance and infrastructure bottlenecks. He warns that the U.S. is dangerously unprepared for the commodity supercycle driven by AI, EVs, and clean energy.\n    20|\n    21|SAM: The strongest argument is that critical minerals like lithium, cobalt, and rare earths are the new oil—strategic assets that determine technological and military power. Dreyfus argues that the U.S. lacks both domestic production capacity and secure supply chains.\n    22|\n    23|MAYA: A counterpoint: Could recycling, substitution, or new mining technologies offset the current dependency on China? It’s unclear how quickly these alternatives can scale to meet demand.\n    24|\n    25|SAM: Why should DJ care? This is a strategic risk in the AI and energy transition story. If the U.S. can’t secure critical minerals, it risks falling behind in both AI infrastructure and green energy, with cascading effects on national security and tech leadership.\n    26|\n    27|---\n    28|\n    29|### Episode 3: **The Cognitive Revolution** – *Babysitting the Machine: Glean's Rebecca Hinds*\n    30|\n    31|MAYA: Rebecca Hinds from Glean reveals that while 87% of workers use AI, only 13% report improved performance—largely due to “botsitting” and “botshitting.” Hidden labor costs are undermining AI’s promised productivity gains.\n    32|\n    33|SAM: The strongest evidence: A large portion of AI use in the enterprise is actually spent managing, correcting, and babysitting AI outputs rather than gaining efficiency. This contradicts the narrative that AI is already a productivity multiplier.\n    34|\n    35|MAYA: A counterpoint: This may be a temporary phase. As models improve and interfaces evolve, the friction may decrease, and productivity gains could materialize.\n    36|\n    37|SAM: Why should DJ care? This challenges the bullish AI productivity thesis. Investors and operators need to be cautious about overestimating near-term ROI from AI tools in the enterprise, especially without better workflows and user training.\n    38|\n    39|---\n    40|\n    41|### Synthesis\n    42|\n    43|MAYA: These episodes together paint a picture of AI’s strategic importance across infrastructure, supply chains, and enterprise productivity. Huang frames AI as the new electricity—transformative and foundational. Dreyfus warns that without secure access to critical minerals, that transformation is at risk. And Hinds shows that even with all the compute and minerals in the world, AI adoption is bottlenecked by poor user experience and hidden labor costs.\n    44|\n    45|SAM: DJ should care because these are not isolated trends—they’re interdependent. AI’s economic impact depends on hardware access, geopolitical stability in supply chains, and real-world usability. The next wave of value creation will go to those who can align all three.\n    46|", "total_lines": 45, "file_size": 4431, "truncated": false, "is_binary": false, "is_image": false}

{"success": true, "name": "media:podcast-intelligence-digest", "file": "references/local-tts-backends.md", "content": "# Local TTS backend calibration\n\n## Outcome\nEarlier testing found **Piper** was the fastest cost-effective local backend, but DJ later judged both **Piper** and **Edge TTS** too robotic for the weekly digest. Do not treat speed as production quality.\n\n### Tested options\n- **Piper**: fast and reliable locally, but too robotic for DJ's production weekly digest.\n- **Edge TTS**: easy fallback and somewhat better than Piper in places, but still not natural enough.\n- **Kokoro**: local option now under active calibration; `af_heart` was judged good for Maya/expert, and alternate Sam/interlocutor voices are being tested.\n- **Chatterbox**: installable locally, but CPU synthesis was too slow for weekly production use in this environment.\n\n## Practical recommendation\nFor production-quality weekly audio, prefer a truly natural voice stack. If premium credentials/gateway are available, test ElevenLabs/OpenAI/Gemini/xAI/Mistral before spending more time tweaking Piper/Edge. If staying local, continue Kokoro calibration first.\n\n## Working Piper recipe\n1. Create or use an isolated Python 3.11 environment for TTS tooling.\n2. Install `piper-tts` into that environment.\n3. Download voices with `python -m piper.download_voices <voice> --download-dir <dir>`.\n4. Prefer distinct voices for MAYA/SAM rather than relying on rate/pitch changes alone.\n5. Render to WAV, then concatenate/encode to Opus for Telegram delivery.\n\n## Useful local voices\n- Kokoro MAYA/expert: `af_heart` (approved as good by DJ)\n- Kokoro SAM/interlocutor candidates tested: `am_echo`, `am_eric`, `am_fenrir`, `am_liam`, `am_michael`, `am_onyx`, `am_puck`, `bm_george`, `bm_lewis`\n- Piper voices below are fallback/testing only, not production-approved:\n  - `en_US-hfc_female-medium`\n  - `en_US-hfc_male-medium`\n  - `en_US-lessac-medium`\n\n## Notes\n- Chatterbox may require patching around its watermarking dependency if used as a local experiment.\n- Kokoro's quality is decent, but its CPU workflow was too slow for the weekly show compared with Piper.\n- If a future environment has a stronger GPU-backed local voice stack, re-evaluate, but do not assume heavier models are better for production cost/performance.\n", "file_type": ".md"}
```
